->By CHARLES HOOLE, Teacher of a Private Grammar-School in *Lothbury,* LONDON.<-

### THE
# Author’s PREFACE
### TO THE
# READER.

<image-desc>Large decorative drop-cap "I" set within a square woodcut border featuring floral and foliage motifs.</image-desc>

Instruction is the means to expel *unrefined ignorance* <note>original: "Rudeness." In the 17th century, "rudeness" referred to a lack of education, culture, or "polishing" of the mind.</note>; with which young minds <note>original: "Wits."</note> ought to be well-furnished in schools: But in such a way that the teaching be 1. *True*, 2. *Full*, 3. *Clear*, and 4. *Solid*.

1. It will be *true*, if nothing is taught except that which is beneficial to one's life; lest there be a cause for complaining afterwards. We do not know necessary things because we have not learned the things that are necessary. <note>This is a paraphrase of a famous sentiment by the Roman philosopher Seneca: "Non vitae sed scholae discimus" (We learn not for life but for the schoolroom).</note>

2. It will be *full*, if the mind is polished for wisdom, the tongue for eloquence, and the hands for a refined way of living. This will be that *grace of one's life: to be wise, to act, and to speak*.

3. 4. It will be *clear*, and by that firm and *solid*, if whatever is taught and learned is not obscure or confused, but apparent, distinct, and articulate, as the fingers on the hands.

The ground of this business is that *sensual ob-* <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "jects" indicates the full term is "sensual objects," meaning things that can be perceived by the five senses.</note>

<vocab>Instruction, Ignorance, Schools, Wisdom, Eloquence, Teaching</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces his educational philosophy, arguing that teaching must be practical, comprehensive, and clear to effectively refine a student's mind and character.</summary>
<keywords>education, pedagogy, wisdom, eloquence, 17th-century schooling, Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical justification for sensory-based learning, emphasizing that knowledge must enter through the senses before it can reach the intellect.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

objects be rightly presented to the senses, for fear they may not be received. I say, and say it again aloud, that this last is the foundation of all the rest: because we can neither act nor speak wisely, unless we first rightly understand all the things which are to be done, and whereof we are to speak. Now there is nothing in the understanding which was not before in the sense <note>This is a reference to the Aristotelian principle "Nihil est in intellectu quod non prius fuerit in sensu," a cornerstone of empirical philosophy.</note>. And therefore to exercise the senses well about the right perceiving the differences of things, will be to lay the grounds for all wisdom, and all wise discourse, and all discreet actions in one's course of life. Which, because it is commonly neglected in Schools, and the things that are to be learned are offered to Scholars, without being understood, or being rightly presented to the senses, it cometh to pass, that the work of teaching and learning goeth heavily onward, and affordeth little benefit.

See here then a new help for Schools. *A Picture and Nomenclature <note>A system of names or terms used in a particular subject.</note> of all the chief things in the World, and of men's Actions in their way of living!* Which, that you, good Masters, may not be loth to run over with your Scholars, I will tell you in short, what good you may expect from it.

It is a little Book, as you see, of no great bulk, yet a brief of the whole world, and a whole language: full of Pictures, Nomenclatures, and Descriptions of things.

I. The Pictures are the Representations of all visible things, (to which also things invisible are reduced after their fashion) of the whole world. And that in that very order of things, in which

<summary>The author argues that sensory perception is the essential foundation of all understanding and introduces this illustrated book as a tool to fix the failures of traditional schooling. He describes the work as a concise encyclopedia of the world, using images to represent both visible and invisible concepts.</summary>

<keywords>sensory learning, empiricism, pedagogy, visual aids, nomenclature, education reform, Orbis Pictus</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of the book's structure, specifically how the "Janua" (Gate) of language is integrated with visual illustrations to assist the learner's understanding.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

they are described in the *Gate of the Latin Language* <note>original: "Ianna Latina Lingua" (likely a typo for Ianua); this refers to Comenius's famous textbook, *The Door of Languages Unlocked*.</note>, and with that fullness, that nothing very necessary or of great concernment is omitted.

II. *The Nomenclatures* <term>Nomenclature: A system of names or terms used in a particular subject.</term> are the inscriptions, or titles set every one over their own pictures, expressing the whole thing by its own general term.

III. *The Descriptions* are the explanations of the parts of the picture, so expressed by their own proper terms, as that same figure <note>A reference number or letter in the illustration.</note> which is added to every piece of the picture, and the term of it, always showeth what things belong one to another.

Which such book, and in such a dress may (I hope) serve,

I. To entice *clever children* <note>The original uses "witty," which in the 17th century meant intelligent or quick-minded.</note> to it, that they may not imagine a torment to be in the school, but "dainty-fare" <note>A metaphor for a delicious or pleasant feast.</note>. For it is apparent, that children (even from their infancy almost) are delighted with pictures, and willingly please their eyes with these sights. And it will be very well worth the pains to have once brought it to pass, that "scarecrows" <note>A reference to the harsh, frightening methods of traditional 17th-century schooling.</note> may be taken away out of wisdom's gardens.

II. This same little book will serve to stir up the *Attention*, which is to be fastened upon things, and ever to be sharpened more and more; which is also a great matter. For the senses (being the main guides of childhood, because therein the mind does not as yet raise itself up to an abstracted contemplation of things) evermore seek their own objects, and if they be away, they grow dull, and wander.

<summary>The author explains that the book uses labeled pictures and descriptions to make learning pleasant rather than a "torment." He argues that because children are naturally drawn to images, this method will focus their attention and better prepare their minds for abstract thought.</summary>

<keywords>pedagogy, visual learning, Comenius, sensory education, Latin instruction, childhood development, educational reform</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on the psychological benefits of using visual aids in education. # THE PREFACE.

they turn and twist themselves this way and that, out of a weariness of themselves; but when their objects are present, they grow merry, become lively, and willingly allow themselves to be fixed upon them, until the thing be sufficiently understood. This book then will do a good piece of service in capturing (especially flickering) minds <note>The original uses "Wits," referring to the intellect or attention span of children.</note>, and preparing them for deeper studies.

III. From this, a third benefit will follow: that *children, being won over by this approach and drawn in by this method of observation, may be equipped with knowledge of the primary things in the world through play and cheerful pastime.* In a word, this book will serve to make the use of the *Vestibulum* <term>The Porch: An introductory textbook by Comenius intended to precede the more difficult Latin grammars.</term> and the *Janua Linguarum* <term>The Gate of Languages: Comenius’s famous textbook that taught Latin through descriptions of the world.</term> more pleasing, which was its main purpose from the beginning. Yet, if anyone desires that it be bound in their native tongues also, it offers three other benefits of its own.

I. *First, it will provide a method for learning to read more easily than has been possible until now; especially by having a Symbolical Alphabet placed before it. This includes the characters of the various letters alongside the image of the creature whose voice that letter attempts to imitate, pictured next to it.* <note>Comenius’s "Symbolical Alphabet" used onomatopoeia, such as a picture of a crow saying "ah ah" for the letter A.</note> For the young beginner <note>Original: "Abc Scholar."</note> will easily remember the sound of every character simply by looking at the creature, until the imagination is strengthened by use and can readily recall all things. And then, having looked over a *table of the most important syllables as well (which was not considered necessary to include in this book)*, he may proceed to viewing the pictures and the inscriptions <note>The titles or labels.</note> set over them.

<summary>Comenius explains how visual learning prevents boredom in children and introduces his "Symbolical Alphabet," which uses animal sounds to help beginners master the alphabet.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, visual learning, Symbolical Alphabet, literacy, Vestibulum, Janua Linguarum, early childhood education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of how a "Symbolical Alphabet" helps children learn to read. # THE PREFACE.

them. In this way, simply looking at the pictured object suggests its name, which will show the child how the title of the picture should be read. Thus, by going through the whole book using only the titles of the pictures, the ability to read will inevitably be acquired. Indeed—and this is a point to be noted—this happens *without using the usual tedious method of spelling, that most troublesome torture of the mind* <note>The original uses "wits," referring here to a child's mental faculty or intellect.</note>, which can be completely avoided by this method. For by frequently reading through the book, including those longer descriptions of things placed after the pictures, a habit of reading will be perfectly established.

II. The same book, *when used in English in English schools, will serve for the perfect learning of the entire English language, starting from the very foundation;* because through the aforementioned descriptions of things, the words and phrases of the whole language are found arranged in their proper order. A short English grammar could be added at the end to clearly break down the speech already understood into its parts, showing the declining <note>The grammatical variation of the form of a word; Comenius suggests that seeing words in context makes learning their forms easier.</note> of various words and organizing those joined together under certain rules.

III. From there, a new benefit arises: *that very English translation may serve for a more ready and pleasant learning of the Latin language.* As seen in this edition, the entire book is translated so that in every instance, one word corresponds to the word positioned opposite it <note>The original book was printed in parallel columns, with Latin on one side and English on the other.</note>. The book is the same in all respects, only in two languages <note>original: "idiomes"</note>, like a man wearing a double garment. And there might also—

<meta>This page continues the author's introductory remarks regarding the structure of the book, specifically how it addresses the differences between Latin and English, and provides instructions for its use in early education.</meta>

### THE PREFACE.

...so some Observations and <term>Advertisements: notices, instructions, or explanatory notes</term> added in the end, touching those things only, wherein the use of the Latin tongue differeth from the English. For, where there is no difference, there needeth no advertisement to be given. But, because the first tasks of *Learners ought to be little, and single*, we have filled this first Book of training one up to see a thing of himself with nothing but <term>rudiments: the most basic principles or foundations of a subject</term>, that is, with the chief of things and words, or with the grounds of the whole world, and the whole language, and of all our Understanding about things. If a more perfect description of things, and a fuller knowledge of a language, and a clearer light of the understanding be sought after (as they ought to be) they are to be found some where else, whither there will now be an easy passage by this our *little Encyclopedia* <note>original: "encyclopaedia" — Comenius refers to this book as a "circle of knowledge" designed for children.</note> of things subject to the senses. Something remaineth to be said, touching the more cheerful use of this Book.

I. Let it be given to children into their hands to delight themselves withal as they please, with the sight of the Pictures, and making them as familiar to themselves as may be, and that even at home, before they be put to School.

II. Then let them be examined ever and anon (especially now in the School) what this thing or that thing is, and is called, so that they may see nothing, which they know not how to name, and that they can name nothing, which they cannot show.

III. And let the things named to them be showed,

It provides specific instructions for parents and teachers to encourage children to explore the book's illustrations before formal schooling begins.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the instructions for the teacher and student, completing point III and providing points IV and V of the author's pedagogical advice before transitioning into the main text.</meta>

### THE PREFACE.

not only in the Picture, but *also* in themselves; for example, the parts of the Body, Clothes, Books, the House, Utensils, etc. <note>original: "&c."</note>

IV. Let them be suffered also to imitate the Pictures by hand, if they will; nay rather, let them be encouraged, that they may be willing: first, thus to quicken the attention also towards the things; and to observe the proportion of the parts one towards another; and lastly, to practice the nimbleness of the hand, which is good for many things.

V. If any things here mentioned cannot be presented to the eye, it will be to no purpose at all to offer them by themselves to the <term>Scholars: students or pupils</term>, such as colors, <term>relishes: flavors or tastes</term>, etc., which cannot here be pictured out with ink. For which reason it were to be wished that things rare and not easy to be met with at home might be kept ready in every great School, that they may be showed also as often as any words are to be made of them to the Scholars.

Thus at the last this School would indeed become a School of things obvious to the senses, and an Entrance to the <term>School Intellectual: the higher level of education focused on abstract reasoning and logic</term>. But enough: let us come to the thing itself.

<meta>This page transitions from the author's preface—which focused on how to use the book's illustrations—to the translator's personal address to fellow educators. # THE PREFACE

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative band of repeating floral printer's ornaments, resembling stylized fleurs-de-lis.</image-desc>

## The Translator to all judicious, and industrious School-Masters.

*Gentlemen,*

I believe there are few among you who have not seen, and willingly used (or at least examined), many of the books by this well-deserving author, **Mr. John Comenius**; because of their usefulness in the quick mastery of a language, these works have been translated in various countries from Latin into their own native tongues.

Now, the general verdict—passed after practical trial—concerning those books previously available is this: they are indeed of extraordinary use and very beneficial to those with more <term>discretion: maturity or the ability to judge well</term> (especially to those who already have a basic knowledge of Latin) to help their memories retain what they have picked up here and there, and to provide them with many words which, perhaps, they had not previously read or carefully observed. But for young children (whom we are primarily tasked to teach), who are entirely unfamiliar with most things and words, these books prove to be more of a toil and a burden than a delight and a <term>furtherance: a help to progress</term>.

For to cram the memory with many words for things not yet understood by the mind is to fill the head with empty imaginations, and to make the learner overwhelmed by their sheer number and variety (and thus become discouraged), rather than encouraging them to treasure these words

<meta>catchword: up,</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the translator addresses schoolmasters, praising Comenius’s previous works but arguing that they were too difficult for young children. He suggests that teaching words without connecting them to understood concepts is a burden that discourages early learners.</summary>
<keywords>John Comenius, pedagogy, language acquisition, Latin instruction, Charles Hoole, rote learning, educational theory</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, finishing a sentence about students memorizing words in hopes of eventually understanding them. The translator argues that Comenius has improved his method by focusing on sensory learning before abstract grammar.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

up, in hopes to gain more knowledge of what they mean.

He has therefore in some of his later works seemed to move backward <note>The translator suggests Comenius "moved backward" by returning to the most basic levels of education to help beginners.</note>, and striven to come closer to the reach of young minds; and in this present book, he has (in my opinion) descended to the very basics of what is to be taught, and proceeded (just as nature itself does) in an orderly way: first to exercise the senses well by presenting physical objects to them, and then to take hold of the intellect by impressing the first ideas of things upon it, and linking them one to another through logical reasoning. In contrast, because we generally fail to follow this path, we teach children—just as we do parrots—to speak without knowing what they are saying. Nay, what is worse, by taking the path of teaching *young children through grammar alone at first, we confuse their imaginations with abstract terms and <term>secondary intentions: a technical term in logic for concepts that refer to other words or mental concepts rather than to real-world objects</term>, which they cannot understand until they are somewhat familiar with the things themselves and the words belonging to them in the language they are learning.* 

And I suspect this is why many prominent people sometimes decide not to send a child to school until he is at least eleven or twelve years old, assuming that having observed most things by then, he will more quickly learn the words applied to them in any language. However, the bad behavior of such children has mostly taught many parents to be quick enough to send their own to school—if not so that they may learn, then (at least) so that they may be kept out of 

<meta>catchword: harms;</meta>

<summary>The translator explains that Comenius’s new method follows the natural order of learning by engaging the senses before the intellect. He criticizes the "parrot-like" memorization of grammar rules, arguing that children learn better when they first understand the physical objects that words represent.</summary>

<keywords>John Comenius, pedagogy, sensory learning, grammar instruction, educational reform, child development, secondary intentions</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface. It completes the thought from the previous page regarding parents who send their children to school simply to keep them supervised, even if the children are too young to learn effectively.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

harm's way; and yet, if they do not make progress during the time they have spent at school (regardless of their age), the teacher will certainly be the one to take the blame.

Thus, a schoolmaster must focus his mind <note>original: "bend his wits," meaning to apply one's intelligence intently</note> to reach the level of a child's understanding at six or seven years old (since children that young are now commonly brought to our grammar schools to learn Latin). He must ensure they learn with as much joy and willingness as he himself would teach with skill and ease. At this time, I know of no better resource to help young students progress than this little book, which the author originally designed for this purpose in the German and Latin languages.

The author has already explained in his own preface how this book may be used profitably in his own country and language. However, I will explain how we might use it here in our grammar schools, now that it has been translated into English. As usual, I leave it to every man's discretion and freedom to use it or reject it as he sees fit. As soon as a child can read English perfectly and is brought to school to learn Latin, I would recommend that they be given this book along with their <term>Accidence: a basic textbook covering the fundamentals of grammar, specifically the inflections of words</term>. In this book, the student should practice at least once a day (in addition to his grammar lessons) in the following way:

*I. Let him look over the pictures along with their general titles or captions <note>original: "inscriptions"</note> until he can easily find any one of them and say its name in either English or Latin. In this way, he will grasp the* *method*

<summary>The translator argues that teachers must adapt to the capacity of young children and introduces this bilingual picture book as a tool to make Latin learning more delightful. He recommends using it alongside basic grammar lessons once a child is fluent in reading English.</summary>
<keywords>early childhood education, grammar school, Latin instruction, Charles Hoole, Comenius, visual learning, Accidence, educational method</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator Charles Hoole’s preface to the English edition of Comenius's "Orbis Sensualium Pictus." The previous page introduced the first step of the method: having a child look at the pictures and their titles to understand the overall organization of the book.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

method of the book in his head. In this way, he will be easily provided with knowledge of most things and instructed on what to call them whenever he encounters them elsewhere in their real forms.

II. Let him read the *full description*, first in *English* and afterwards in *Latin*, until he can readily read and clearly pronounce the words in both languages, always paying attention to how they are spelled. Along with this, let him take notice of the figures <note>referring to the numbers printed in the text that correspond to parts of the illustration</note> inserted in the text, and to which part of the picture they point by their matching numbers, until he is well able to find every specific thing by himself and name it instantly, in either English or Latin. Thus, he shall not only learn the most basic <note>original: "primitive," meaning fundamental root words</note> words, but also be intelligently grounded in <term>Orthography: the study of correct spelling according to established usage</term>. This is a subject too generally neglected by us; partly because our English-speaking schools think that children should learn it during Latin lessons, and our Latin schools suppose they have already learned it in English school. It is also partly because our common grammar textbooks are too defective in this area, and students are so little practiced in it that they move from schools to the universities and return from there (some of them) less able to write correct English than either Latin or Greek. This is to say nothing of our ordinary tradesmen, many of whom write such incorrect English that no one but themselves can interpret what they scribble in their bills and accounting ledgers <note>original: "Shop-Books"</note>.

III. Then let him learn the *titles* and *descriptions* by heart. He will do this more easily because of those visual impressions made by viewing the

<summary>The translator outlines the second step of his teaching method: reading descriptions in both English and Latin to master pronunciation and "orthography" (spelling). He laments the poor state of English literacy among university graduates and tradesmen, suggesting this visual method will fix the problem.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Charles Hoole’s preface, moving from the initial reading of words to the more technical aspects of grammar and translation. It follows the instructions on how a child should first familiarize themselves with the pictures and basic vocabulary before moving into the structural rules of Latin.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

images already formed in his memory. And now let him also learn:

1. **To construe**, or provide the words one by one, as they correspond to each other in Latin and English. 

2. **To parse**, according to the rules (which I assume by now) he has learned in the first part of the <term>Accidence: the introductory part of grammar dealing with the inflections of words, such as how endings change</term>; where I would have him **identify what part of speech any word is, and then which "accidents" belong to it** <note>In historical grammar, "accidents" refer to properties like gender, number, case, or tense</note>; but especially to **decline** the nouns and **conjugate** the verbs according to the examples in his **Rudiments** <note>the basic principles found in his introductory textbook</note>. Doing this will enable him to understand the purpose and use of his grammar lessons. 

**As for the rules concerning the genders of nouns, the past-perfect tenses and "supines" <note>a specific type of Latin verbal noun</note> of verbs, or those rules of agreement and sentence structure found in the latter part of the grammar book, I would not have a child much troubled with them** until, with the help of this book, he can **perfectly practice** as much of <term>Etymology: here referring to the study of word forms and their grammatical classifications</term> as concerns the first part of his grammar book only. 

For that part of grammar and this book together, being thoroughly learned by going over them at least three times, will greatly prepare children to move forward cheerfully in their formal grammar and the classical school authors—especially if, while they are occupied with this, they are also taught to write a fair and legible hand.

There is one thing to be noted, which I wish could have been remedied in this translation: the book was originally written in **High German** <note>original: "high-Dutch," the standard term for German in the 17th century</note> and describes many things specific to that country and language. These things cannot be expressed in our language without altering some of the pictures as well as the words. For example, the **Symbolical Alphabet** is

<meta>The text cuts off here at the catchword "fitted," which begins the next page's discussion on how the alphabet was adapted from German to English sounds.</meta>

<summary>This page details the second step of the educational method, focusing on parsing, declining nouns, and conjugating verbs. Hoole also notes the difficulties in translating the work from German to English, particularly regarding the "Symbolical Alphabet."</summary>

<keywords>grammar instruction, parsing, Latin pedagogy, translation challenges, German to English, Charles Hoole, Orbis Pictus, Symbolical Alphabet</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Charles Hoole. He is explaining how English teachers should adapt this book, which was originally designed for German speakers, and sharing his personal motivation for supporting this pictorial method of teaching.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

...fitted for German children rather than for ours. And whereas the words of that language go orderly one-for-one with the Latin, our English way of speaking will not admit the same. Therefore it will be necessary for those masters who intend to make use of this book to translate it <term>word-for-word: verbatim</term> <note>original: *verbatim*</note> to their young scholars, who will quickly learn to do it themselves after they are once acquainted with the first words of *nouns* and *verbs*, and their manner of variation.

*Such a work as this*, I observe to have been formerly *much desired* by some experienced teachers, and I myself had some years since (*while my own child lived*) begun a similar project. I had found it most *agreeable to the most intelligent children*, who are *most captivated by pictures* from their infancy, *because by them the knowledge of things, which they seem to represent* (and of which children are as yet ignorant) *is most easily conveyed to their understanding*. But since the work is now done (though in some things not so completely as one might wish), I rejoice in the use of it and stop my own undertakings for the present. And because *any good thing is better when it is more widely shared*, I have here imitated a child, who is eager to give to others what he himself has liked. 

You, then, who have the care of little children: do not too much trouble their thoughts or clog their memories with bare grammar basics, which for them are harsh in the learning and easily lost in the retaining; because, indeed, to them they signify nothing, but...

<sig>(b)</sig>
<meta>catchword: a</meta>

<summary>Charles Hoole discusses the challenges of translating Latin into English versus German and argues that children learn better through pictures than through dry grammatical rules. He reveals he once started a similar project while his own child was alive but has set it aside in favor of this completed work.</summary>
<keywords>Latin pedagogy, translation methods, Charles Hoole, visual learning, grammar instruction, childhood education, Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Charles Hoole’s preface. He is concluding his argument that abstract grammar rules are ineffective if a child does not first understand the physical objects and specific words those rules describe.</meta>

# THE PREFACE.

<sig>(d)</sig>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

...a vague or unstable idea of a general category <note>original: "swimming notion of a general term"</note>, which they do not understand until they also grasp the specific examples. Instead, by this or a similar supporting aid <term>subsidiary: a helpful resource or tool</term> <note>original: *Subsidiarie*</note>, first provide them with some knowledge of actual objects and the words to describe them; then, their rules for speaking will be better understood and more firmly remembered. Otherwise, how should a child understand what a rule means when he knows neither what the Latin <note>original: *Latine*</note> word signifies, nor what kind of thing it is that is being explained to him in his own native language—which is given to him specifically to help him understand the rule? For rules, being made of generalities, are delivered (so to speak) at a third-hand removal; they assume that the things and the words are already understood, even though the rules are based upon them. I could indeed speak at greater length on this subject, *as it is the very foundation of our profession to investigate how children gradually grasp what we teach them, so that we may adapt ourselves to their level of understanding. However, I leave the observation of this to your own daily practice and the experience you gain from it.*

And I pray to God, the source and giver of all wisdom who has granted us this <term>gift of teaching</term>, to so inspire and guide us by his grace that we may raise children in his fear and in the knowledge of his Son Jesus Christ <note>original: *Jesus Christ*</note> our Lord. Then, without doubt, our teaching and their learning of other subjects that are secondary to these will—with the help of his Holy Spirit—make them both able and willing to serve him faithfully in both the Church and

<summary>Charles Hoole concludes his preface by arguing that children must understand physical objects and basic vocabulary before being burdened with abstract grammar rules. He ends with a prayer for teachers to successfully guide their students in both practical knowledge and Christian faith.</summary>

<keywords>pedagogy, language acquisition, Charles Hoole, visual learning, Latin instruction, religious education, early childhood development</keywords>

> Certainly the *use of Images, or Representations*
> *is great:* If we could make our words as easy to
> read <note>original: "legible"</note> to children as pictures are, their learning
> from them would be faster <note>original: "quickened"</note> and more certain. But

# A Special <note>original: "Particular"</note> Advertisement.

Because several gentlemen of high social standing <note>original: "good quality"</note> have previously been very eager for **Charles Hoole** to both board <note>original: "Table"</note> and teach their children, this is to give notice to everyone—but especially to those who know him—that he has now prepared a house with all the necessary comforts to properly accommodate them. He will be diligent in his work and will also employ a capable assistant <term>Usher: a junior or assistant teacher in a school</term> in his house to oversee their lessons. His wife and her servants will also be ready to ensure the children receive a healthy diet and are kept clean and orderly, for a fee <note>original: "Rate"</note> that reflects the great expense and effort required for such a task. Located at the *North-East Corner* of the *New Buildings* in *Token-house Garden* in *Lothbury, London, near the Royal Exchange.* <note>Token-house Garden was an area in the City of London named after the building where copper farthing tokens were minted.</note>

---

**There is now in the Press, a third Edition, corrected and enlarged, of** *The Easy Entrance to the Latin Language* <note>original: "Of the easie Entrance to the Latine Tongue"</note>. **Written by Mr. Charles Hoole, containing:**

1. The basics <note>original: "Rudiments"</note> of grammar, along with an examination of word forms <term>Accidence: the part of grammar dealing with the inflections of words, such as how nouns change for plural or verbs change for tense</term>.
2. A vocabulary of common words, in English and Latin.
3. Various short examples that apply the rules of agreement <term>Concordance: the grammatical agreement between words, such as a verb matching its subject</term> and sentence structure <term>Construction: the arrangement of words to form meaningful sentences; syntax</term>.
4. Collections taken from the authors used in the introductory school levels.
5. More refined <note>original: "Elegant"</note> expressions for children to use.
6. The first principles of Christianity. These may be purchased bound together as one volume, or separately, as instructed in the Preface.

# Invitation. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Invitation. <note>original: "Invitatio"</note>

---

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration of a landscape with various features marked with numbers. On the far left, a rock formation with a small building or figure (16) sits in the water near other jagged rocks (11, 9). A mountain peak (12) overlooks a central flat area or lake (4). ->The Master, and the Boy.<-

M. **C**ome Boy! learn to be wise.
P. What does this mean, to be wise?
M. To understand rightly, to do rightly, and to speak out rightly, all that are necessary.
P. Who will teach me this?
M. I, by God's help.
P. How

<column-break/>

->The Master and the Boy.<- <note>original: "Magister & Puer"</note>

*M. **C**ome, Boy! learn to be wise.* <note>original: "Veni, Puer! disce Sapere."</note>
*P. What is this, to be wise?* <note>original: "Quid hoc est, Sa-pere?"</note>
*M. To understand everything rightly, to act rightly, and to speak out rightly, which are necessary.* <note>original: "Omnia, quæ ne-cessaria, rectè intelligere, rectè agere, rectè elo-qui."</note>
*P. Who will teach me this?* <note>original: "Quis me hoc doce-bit?"</note>
*M. I, with God.* <note>original: "Ego, cum DEO."</note>
*P. In what way?* <note>original: "Quomodo?"</note>

<note>This dialogue establishes the "Comenian" method: education is the pursuit of wisdom through understanding the world, acting correctly, and communicating clearly.</note>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Comenius's educational masterpiece, featuring a landscape woodcut and a dialogue where a Master invites a Boy to learn wisdom through the senses. The lower portion continues a list of corrections for various errors found in the printed text.</summary>
<keywords>Invitation, Master, Pupil, Wisdom, Orbis Pictus, Education, Sense Perception, Woodcut, Typographical Errors, Charles Hoole, Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Invitation" from the previous page. The Master (M) promises to guide the Boy (P) through the entire world, starting with the basic sounds of speech modeled after animal noises. This reflects Comenius's pedagogical theory that learning should proceed from the simple (sounds) to the complex (the world) and from the senses (hearing and seeing) to the intellect.</meta>

( 3 )
B 2

M. I will guide you through all things.

I will show you all things.

I will name all things for you.

P. See, here I am; lead me in the name of God.

M. Before all things, you ought to learn the plain sounds, of which human speech consists; which living creatures know how to make, and your tongue knows how to imitate, and your hand can draw. <note>The original English uses "picture out" for the Latin "pingere" (to paint or draw).</note>

Afterwards we will go into the world, and we will view all things.

Here you have a living and vocal Alphabet.

<column-break/>

M. I will lead you through all things, <note>original: "Ducam te, per omnia"</note>

I will show you all things. <note>original: "Ostendam tibi omnia"</note>

I will name all things for you. <note>original: "Nominabo tibi omnia"</note>

P. Behold, I am here; lead me, in the name of GOD. <note>original: "En adsum; duc me, in nomine DEI"</note>

M. Before all things, you ought to learn the simple sounds, from which *human speech* consists; which, *Animals* know how to form, and your *Tongue* knows how to imitate, and your *Hand* can draw. <note>original: "Ante omnia, debes discere simplices Sonos, ex quibus constat Sermo humanus; quos, Animalia sciunt formare, & tua Lingua scit imitari, & tua Manus potest pingere."</note>

Afterward we will go into the *World*, and we will see all things. <note>original: "Postea ibimus in Mundum, & spectabimus omnia."</note>

You have here a *living and vocal Alphabet*. <note>original: "Alphabetum vivum & vocale habes hîc."</note>

<note>original: "guide, sounds, speech, living Creatures, tongue, hand, Alphabet, Mundum (World), Sermo (Speech/Discourse), Animalia (Animals)"</note>

<summary>The Master concludes his invitation by promising to name and show the Boy everything in the world, beginning with a "living alphabet" based on sounds made by animals. This establishes the foundation for the sensory-based learning that defines the rest of the book.</summary>

<keywords>phonics, imitation, nature, education, human speech, alphabet, Comenius, sensory learning</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page begins the "Vocal Alphabet," a cornerstone of Comenius's pedagogical method. Following the introduction on page 3, the teacher now presents the sounds of the alphabet by mimicking the natural noises of animals and the environment. This sensory-driven approach was revolutionary for the 17th century, aiming to teach children letters through the sounds they already recognize in the world around them.</meta>

<meta>This page marks the transition from the "Vocal Alphabet" (the sounds of letters) to the formal lessons of the *Orbis Pictus*. Following the educational philosophy of the time, Comenius begins his "encyclopedia of the world" with the Creator. This page establishes the theological framework for the rest of the book, defining God’s nature before proceeding to the creation of the world.</meta>

## II.

### God <note>original Latin: *Deus*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram of the "Shield of Faith" (Latin: *Scutum Fidei*), a traditional visual representation of the Trinity. The central circle contains the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (יהוה), the four-letter name of God. Radiating from this center are three paths leading to outer circles labeled "FATHER" <note>original: *PATER*</note>, "SON" <note>original: *FILIVS*</note>, and "HOLY SPIRIT" <note>original: *SPS SANCTVS*</note>. The paths between the center and the outer circles are labeled "is" <note>original: *est*</note>, signifying that each person is God. The paths connecting the outer circles to each other are labeled "is not" <note>original: *non est*</note>, signifying that the Father is not the Son, and so on. The entire diagram is surrounded by a radiant sunburst representing divine light.</image-desc>

<term>Vesperi</term>: In the evening.
<term>Crepusculum</term>: Twilight; the faint light before sunrise or after sunset.
<term>Aurora / Diluculum</term>: Dawn and daybreak; the first appearance of light in the morning.
</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the geocentric movement of the heavens around a stationary Earth, explaining how the Sun's rays create day and how its absence results in night. It identifies the celestial bodies—Sun, Moon, and Stars—and the transitional periods of twilight and dawn.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, geocentrism, Sun, Moon, Stars, day and night cycle, twilight, dawn, Latin-English vocabulary</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the celestial realm described on page 11—where the Sun, Moon, and stars govern light and time—to the terrestrial elements, starting with Fire. It provides a practical, 17th-century look at how fire is generated and utilized in a domestic setting.</meta>

# V.

## Fire. <note>original: *Ignis*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a room with a tiled floor. In the upper left, a window looks out onto a landscape where a large column of smoke and fire rises (labeled 8). To the right is a large fireplace with a flame (7) and a column of smoke (9) rising up the chimney (10). A stick or piece of wood (6) is near the fire.</image-desc>

The **Fire**
gloweth, burneth, and
consumeth to ashes.
A spark of it struck
out of a **Flint** 1
(or **Firestone**) 2.
by means of a **Steel** 1.
and taken by **tinder** <note>original: *fomite*</note> in
a **tinder-box** 3.
lighteth a **match** 4 <note>original: *sulphuratum* — specifically a sulfur-dipped wooden splint used to transfer a spark</note>
and after that
a **Candle** 5,
or a **Stick**, 6.
and causeth
a **flame** 7.

glows, burns,
and consumes.

struck from the **Flint (Firestone)** 2,

or the **Wood** 6
is lit,
and stirs up a **Flame** 7.

| Original Term | English Translation | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| <term>Incendium</term> | **Blaze / Conflagration** | A large, destructive fire, specifically one that "seizes" buildings. |
| <term>Fumus</term> | **Smoke** | The visible vapor produced by something burning. |
| <term>Camino</term> | **Chimney** | The flue or passage through which smoke escapes a house. |
| <term>Fuliginem</term> | **Soot** | The black powdery substance (carbon) that sticks to the chimney. |
| <term>Torre</term> | **Fire-brand** | A piece of wood that is currently burning. |
| <term>Titis</term> | **Brand** | A piece of wood that was burning but has been extinguished. |
| <term>Prunâ</term> | **Live Coal** | A red-hot, glowing ember or piece of wood. |
| <term>Carbo</term> | **Coal / Charcoal** | The black, "dead" remains of a piece of wood after the fire is out. |
| <term>Cinis / Favilla</term> | **Ash / Embers** | The final powdery residue of combustion; *favilla* refers specifically to ashes that are still hot. |

<summary>This page details the effects of fire on structures and wood, categorizing the various states of burning matter from active blazes and smoke to extinguished brands, charcoal, and ashes.</summary>
<keywords>fire, smoke, soot, chimney, fire-brand, charcoal, ashes, embers, domestic life, 17th century science</keywords>

| Term | English Translation | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| <term>Incendium</term> | **Blaze / Conflagration** | A large, destructive fire, specifically one that "seizes" buildings. |
| <term>Fumus</term> | **Smoke** | The visible vapor and gases produced by something burning. |
| <term>Camino</term> | **Chimney** | The flue or passage through which smoke escapes a building. |
| <term>Fuliginem</term> | **Soot** | The black, powdery carbon deposit that clings to the chimney walls. |
| <term>Torre</term> | **Fire-brand** | A piece of wood that is actively burning with flame. |
| <term>Titis</term> | **Brand** | A piece of wood that was burning but has been put out (quenched). |
| <term>Prunâ</term> | **Live Coal** | A glowing, red-hot fragment of fuel that has no visible flame. |
| <term>Carbo</term> | **Coal / Charcoal** | The cold, black remains of burnt wood or fuel. |
| <term>Cinis</term> | **Ash** | The fine, grey powdery residue left after something is completely burnt. |
| <term>Favilla</term> | **Embers** | Small, hot, glowing particles found within the ashes. |

# XXIX.

## Wild Beasts. <note>original: "Feræ pecudes"; literally "wild cattle," referring to large four-footed wild animals.</note>

### XXXV.
### Marine Fish, and Shells.

<note>The original Latin title is "Marini Pisces, & Conchae."</note>

### Sea-Fish, and Shell-Fish.

---

The **Whale** (1) <note>original: "Balaena"</note> is the greatest of the Sea-fish. The **Dolphin** (2) is the swiftest. The **Ray** (3) is the most monstrous. Others are the **Sturgeon** (4), the **Salmon** (5), and the **Pike** (6).

<column-break/>

The **Seal** (9) <note>literally "Sea-calf"</note> lives both in the water and on the land. **Shell-fish** (10) have hard shells, such as the **Oyster** (11), the **Scallop** (12), the **Cockle** (13), and the **Snail-shell** (14). **Pearl-oysters** yield **Pearls**.

<margin>Corals and Sea-plants grow beneath the waves.</margin>

<summary>This page categorizes various marine life, distinguishing between large mammals like whales, common fish, and various types of hard-shelled mollusks. It serves as an introductory guide to marine biology and the diversity of the ocean's inhabitants.</summary>

<keywords>marine biology, whale, dolphin, shellfish, pearls, seal, natural history</keywords>

## XXXVI.
### Man.
<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the Garden of Eden. In the foreground, Eve stands to the left of a tree, reaching for a fruit, with a serpent coiled around the tree trunk. Adam stands to the right, facing Eve, with his arms outstretched. In the background, other figures are visible, some appearing to be working or interacting with the environment. The scene is set amidst trees and foliage.</image-desc>
### Man.
---
<note>The Latin title "Homo" refers to humanity or mankind in a general sense, though the illustration focuses on the specific biblical figures of the first parents.</note>

Man <note>original: "Homo"</note> was created by God on the sixth day, formed from the dust of the earth and animated with a living soul. He was placed in the Garden of Eden, a place of perfect delight, to tend it and keep it. 

<margin>The Fall of Man</margin>

But the Serpent, being more subtle than any beast of the field, deceived the woman, Eve, so that she ate of the forbidden fruit of the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil. She gave also unto her husband, Adam, and he did eat. <insert>Thus sin entered into the world.</insert>

<column-break/>

By this act of disobedience, they were cast out from the Garden, and the ground was cursed for their sake. Man was henceforth to eat his bread by the sweat of his brow until he returns to the earth from which he was taken.

<gloss>Adam signifies "Earth" or "Red Clay" in the ancient tongue</gloss>

| State of Man | Condition |
| :--- | :--- |
| Before the Fall | Innocence and Immortality |
| After the Fall | Labor, Sorrow, and Mortality |

<note>The "sweat of the brow" is a traditional <term>idiom</term> (a common expression) referring to hard physical labor.</note>

<summary>This page describes the creation of Adam and Eve, their placement in the Garden of Eden, and their subsequent fall from grace after being deceived by the serpent.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Eve, Creation, Garden of Eden, Original Sin, Fall of Man, Serpent</keywords>

# XL.

## The Flesh and the Bowels.

## The Flesh and the Internal Organs. <note>original: "Caro & Viscera"</note>

<image-desc>Two anatomical woodcut illustrations are presented side-by-side. The left image depicts a standing male figure, with the skin removed to expose the superficial musculature of the entire body. His arms are raised and slightly bent, gesturing outwards. The right image shows a torso with the chest and abdomen opened to reveal the internal organs, including the brain (visible in the opened skull), lungs, heart, and intestines. The figure is headless below the brain section and armless.</image-desc>

In the Body are the Skin
with the Membranes,
the flesh with the muscles,
the Channels <note>referring to the veins, arteries, and nerves</note>,
the Cartilages <note>original: "Gristles"</note>,
and bones and the bowels.
The Skin being pulled off, the flesh
appears, not in a con-
tinued lump, but being
distributed, as it were
into diverse packings <note>original: "farcimina," literally meaning "sausages" or "stuffings," used here to describe the bundles of muscle fibers</note>
which they call muscles.

In the Body are the Skin
with the Membranes,
the Flesh with the Muscles,
the Channels,
the Cartilages,
the Bones and the Internal Organs.
When the Skin is drawn away,
the Flesh appears <note>The character '2' here is unusual in this context; it might be a printer's error, a stylized 'et' (and), or a reference mark.</note>
not as a continuous mass,
but distributed,
as if into stuffings,
which they call Muscles.

### Monstrous and Deformed People

**Monstrous** <note>original: "Monstrosæ"</note> and **deformed people** <note>original: "& Deformes"</note> are those who differ in their body from the ordinary shape; such as:

The huge **Giant**, 1. <note>original: "immanis Gigas"</note>
The little **Dwarf**, 2. <note>original: "nanus Pumilio"</note>
One with **two bodies**, 3. <note>original: "Bicorpor"</note>
One with **two heads**, 4. <note>original: "Biceps"</note>
And such like **Monsters**.

Amongst these are reckoned:

The **jolt-headed** <note>A person with an unusually large or heavy head; original: "Capito"</note>, 5.
The **great-Nosed**, 6. <note>original: "Naso"</note>
The **blubber-lipped**, 7. <note>original: "Labeo"</note>
The **blub-cheeked** <note>Someone with puffed or swollen cheeks; original: "Bucco"</note>, 8.
The **goggle-eyed** <note>Referring here to someone with a squint or crossed eyes; original: "Strabo"</note>, 9.
The **wry-necked**, 10. <note>original: "Obstipus"</note>
The **great-throated** <note>Likely referring to someone with a goiter or scrofula; original: "Strumosus"</note>, 11.
The **crump-backed** <note>Hunchbacked; original: "Gibbosus"</note>, 12.
The **crump-footed** <note>Club-footed or bow-legged; original: "Loripes"</note>, 13.
The **steeple-crowned** <note>A person with a head that tapers to a point; original: "Cilo"</note>, 15.
Add to these the **Bald-pated**, 14. <note>original: "Calvastrum"</note>

(O)

# XLVIII.
## *Beekeeping.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a beekeeping scene. On the right, a multi-tiered structure holds numerous beehives. In the center, a person (beekeeper) holds a round object (labeled '3') while a swarm of bees (labeled '2') flies above. To the left, three tall, cylindrical beehives (labeled '1') stand. In the foreground, a plate with honeycomb (labeled '5' and '6') rests near a body of water, with a tool (labeled '4') nearby. The background shows hills and clouds.</image-desc>

### The making of Honey.

The **Bees** <note>original: "Apes"</note> dwell in **Bee-hives** (1), which are made of either straw or bark. They fly out in a **Swarm** (2) when a new King <note>The author uses "King," though we now know the leader of the hive is a Queen.</note> leads them forth. They are called back by the ringing of a **brazen Vessel** (3) <note>A common historical belief was that loud metallic noises would encourage a swarm to settle.</note>, and are shut up in a new Hive.

They make **Honey-combs** (5) with six-cornered cells, and fill them with **Honey** (6), which they suck from flowers. From the Honey-combs, the **Wax** is melted by heat, and the Honey is squeezed out.

The **Drones** <note>term: Drones (male bees that do not gather nectar)</note> are those which do not work, and are often expelled by the workers. The **Sting** (4) is the weapon of the Bee, which she leaves in the wound when she pricks.

---
<sig>H</sig>
<sig>I</sig>
<vocab>Beekeeping, Honey, beehives, honeycomb</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the traditional methods of beekeeping, including the housing of bees in hives, the process of swarming, and the extraction of honey and wax from the combs.</summary>
<keywords>beekeeping, honey, wax, beehives, drones, swarming, natural history</keywords>

We have seen
Man's Food <note>original: *Victum*</note>

## LXIV.

### *The Mason.* <note>original: "Faber Murarius"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting masons at work. On the left, a man kneels, mixing mortar in a trough. In the center, a large wall is being constructed. One mason stands on top of the wall, laying bricks, while another operates a pulley system to lift materials. A third mason stands at the base of the wall, possibly preparing bricks or mortar. In the background, there are hills and some trees.</image-desc>

### The Mason.

Whosoever
layest
the foundations
of a building
firmly,
and composest
the structure
of stones
with lime <note>A caustic substance used to make mortar</note>
and cement <note>original: "caemento-que"</note>,
and strengthenest
thy work:
thou art rightly
called
a Mason.
Thou art he
that buildest
houses,
and raisest
walls
to the sky.

<summary>This page describes the craft of the mason, detailing the materials used—such as lime and cement—and the mason's role in laying foundations and raising walls.</summary>
<keywords>masonry, architecture, building materials, lime, mortar, construction trades</keywords>

<meta>catchword: Machine <note>original: Machina</note>.</meta>

<term>Mason (Faber murarius)</term>: A craftsman who works with stone or brick to create structures.
<term>Rough-cast (Tectorio)</term>: A coarse plaster used to cover the exterior of walls, often mixed with small stones or gravel.
<term>Rule (Norma)</term>: A tool used by masons to ensure right angles and straight lines.

<summary>This page describes the tools and materials used by a mason, detailing the process of building walls from either quarried stone or fired bricks. It explains the finishing process of plastering and rough-casting for structural integrity and decoration.</summary>
<keywords>masonry, construction, quarrying, brickmaking, architecture, tools, lime, plaster</keywords>

### LXX.

### *The Potter.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a potter's workshop. On the left, there is a large kiln or oven (11) with smoke rising from it, set into a landscape with hills. Below the kiln, there are some finished pots (12) and a pile of clay (70). On the right, a potter (1) sits on a stool (10) at a potter's wheel (8), shaping a pot from clay. Around him are various finished and unfinished ceramic vessels: a jug (2), a pile of clay (3), a pitcher (4), a bowl (5), a mug (6), and other dishes (7, 9). In the background, there is a window and a tall structure with multiple shelves or openings, possibly for drying or storing pots.</image-desc>

### **The Potter.**

The **Potter** (1) <note>original: "Figulus"</note> sits over the **Potter's Wheel** (8), and makes out of **Clay** (3) <note>original: "Luto"</note> all kinds of **Earthenware** <term>vessels made of baked clay</term>.

First, he takes the **Clay** (70) and tempers it with water; then he places it upon the **Wheel** (8), which he turns with his foot, and with his hands he fashions the shape of the vessel.

He makes **Jugs** (2), **Pitchers** (4), **Bowls** (5), **Mugs** (6), and other **Dishes** (7, 9).

Afterward, he places these vessels in the **Kiln** (11) <note>original: "Furnus"</note> to be baked, so that they may become hard and durable. Those that are to hold liquids, he covers with a **Glaze** <note>original: "Vitro"</note> to make them waterproof.

The broken pieces of pottery are called **Sherds** (12).

<summary>This page describes the traditional craft of the potter, detailing the process of shaping clay on a wheel, firing it in a kiln, and glazing the finished vessels.</summary>
<keywords>pottery, ceramics, kiln, potter's wheel, clay, craftsmanship, industrial arts</keywords>

# LXXI.

<unclear>I. <gloss>The House</gloss></unclear> <note>original: "unlup"; likely a transcription error for "Domus" or a vernacular equivalent for a dwelling.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a cross-section of a multi-story house. The house shows an attic/roof structure at the top, a second floor with two rooms (one with a bed, another with a table and chair), and a ground floor with three rooms (one with a well, another with a table, and a third with an arched entrance and a chest). Various numbers are placed throughout the house, indicating different parts, though most are illegible due to image quality. Visible numbers include 12 (top right), 7 (top right), 17 (bottom right), 18 (bottom right), 19 (bottom right), 20 (bottom right), 21 (bottom right), 22 (bottom right), 23 (bottom right), 24 (bottom right), 25 (bottom right), 26 (bottom right), 27 (bottom right), 28 (bottom right), 29</image-desc>

<column-break/>

The **House** is divided into various parts.
The **Roof** <note>original: "Tectum"</note> covers the top to protect from rain.
Under the roof is the **Garret** or **Attic**, where items are stored.
The **Ceiling** <note>original: "Laquear"</note> is decorated with beams.
The **Stairs** allow one to ascend to the upper floors.
In the **Chamber** <note>original: "Conclave"</note> is the **Bed**, where one rests.
The **Dining Room** <note>original: "Triclinium"</note> contains the **Table** for meals.
On the ground floor, we find the **Court** or **Yard**, often containing a **Well** for water.
The **Threshold** <note>original: "Limen"</note> is at the entrance, beneath the **Door**.
The **Windows** let in the light.
The **Walls** <note>original: "Parietes"</note> support the entire structure.

<summary>This page provides an illustrated anatomical view of a typical early modern house, labeling the various rooms and structural elements from the roof to the ground floor.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, domestic life, house structure, rooms, furniture, 17th-century dwelling</keywords>

### LXXIII.
### Wells.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two buildings and two types of wells. On the left, a two-story building with a large water wheel on its side. In front of this building is a brick well with a crank and bucket, labeled '1'. To the right, a taller, three-story building with multiple windows. To the right of the buildings, in a more open landscape, is a well-sweep (shadoof) structure. A bucket, labeled '3', is suspended from the sweep, being lowered into a wooden well enclosure labeled '2'. Other parts of the mechanism are labeled '4' and '7'. The number '73' is visible below the well on the left.</image-desc>

### Wells.

---

Where the water does not flow of its own accord, <note>original: "non scaturit"</note> it is necessary to dig **Wells** **1**. 

The water is drawn up from the **Well-mouth** **2** <note>The opening at the top of the well shaft.</note> by means of a **Bucket** **3**, which either hangs from a **Well-sweep** <note>original: "Tolleno"; a long counterbalanced pole used to lift water, also known as a shadoof.</note> **4**, or is pulled up by a **Rope** **5** wound around a **Windlass** <note>original: "Sucula"; a horizontal cylinder turned by a crank.</note> **6** by turning a **Handle** or **Crank** **7**.

In other places, water is raised by a **Pump** **8** <note>A more complex mechanical device using suction or pressure.</note> through the use of a **Piston** <note>original: "Embolus"</note>.

## LXXIV.

### The Bath.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting an interior scene of a bathhouse. ### The Bath.

---

<note>original: "Balneum"</note>
The Bathhouse was a place of both hygiene and social gathering. In this scene, we see the various stages of the bathing ritual, which often included washing, scrubbing, and sometimes medical treatments like bloodletting or cupping <note>Common medical practices of the era intended to balance the body's humors</note>. The attendants ensure the water is heated and the patrons are well-served.

# LXXVI.
## The Stable.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a stable. Inside the stable, a horse (labeled '3') stands in a stall, being groomed by a person. To the left of the horse, a manger (labeled '1') is visible, and above it, a hay rack (labeled '2'). A bucket or trough is on the ground near the horse. The number '46' is printed at the bottom left corner of the image.</image-desc>

## The Stable.

<column-break/>

The **Stable** <note>original: "Equile"</note> is the house for horses. The **Groom** <note>original: "Stabularius"</note> or stable-boy cleans it. He puts the fodder into the **Manger** (1) <note>original: "Praesepe"</note> and the hay into the **Rack** (2) <note>original: "Craticula"</note>. He provides water in a **Pail** <note>original: "Aqualis"</note>. He removes the **Dung** <note>original: "Fimus"</note> with a **Pitchfork** <note>original: "Furca"</note> and sweeps the floor with **Brooms** <note>original: "Scopae"</note>. He curries the **Horse** (3) with a **Curry-comb** <note>original: "Strigilis"</note> and wipes him with a **Brush** <note>original: "Penicillum"</note>, then ties him with a **Halter** <note>original: "Funiculus"</note>. He spreads **Litter** <note>original: "Stramentum"</note> or **Straw** <note>original: "Palea"</note> for the horse to sleep on. The **Provender** <note>original: "Pabulum"</note> consists of **Oats** <note>original: "Avena"</note> or **Barley** <note>original: "Hordeum"</note>, along with **Hay** <note>original: "Fænum"</note> and **Dried Straw** <note>original: "Stramen"</note>, which is kept in a **Sack** <note>original: "Saccus"</note> and sifted through a **Sieve** <note>original: "Cribrum"</note>.

<column-break/>

**Stable**; **Groom**; **Manger**; **Rack**; **Water-pail**; **Dung**; **Pitchfork**; **Brooms**; **Curry-comb**; **Brush**; **Halter**; **Litter**; **Chaff**; **Provender**; **Oats**; **Barley**; **Hay**; **Straw**; **Sack**; **Sieve**; **Horse**; **Beast of Burden**; **Colt**; **Gelding**; **Nag**; **Mule**; **Ass**; **Wild Ass**; **Equerry**; **Stable-boy**; **Muleteer**; **Donkey-driver**; **Horse-groom**; **Race-course**; **Centaur** <note>A mythical creature, half-man and half-horse</note>; **Hippogriff** <note>A legendary creature with the front of an eagle and the back of a horse</note>; **Hippopotamus** <note>Literally "River-horse"</note>; **Horse-**...

<keywords>stable, horse care, grooming tools, agriculture, livestock, fodder, centaur, hippogriff</keywords>

### 86.

# *Passing over Waters.* <note>original Latin: "Transitus aquarum"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting various methods of crossing water. In the background, a hilly landscape features a few small houses. In the mid-ground, a person (3) walks across a wooden bridge on the right. In the center, two figures stand on a flat raft (5), maneuvering it with a long pole (9). To the left, a larger ferry or barge (6) is shown, alongside a smaller boat (7) being propelled with oars (8). In the foreground, a man (10) carries a long pole or plank over his shoulder while walking along the shore. The image is annotated with numbers 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, and the printer's mark A5.</image-desc>

# **Passing over Waters.**

---
---

<vocab>Passing over Waters <note>original: "Transitus aquarum"</note>, Passing over Waters, bridge, raft, ferry, boat</vocab>

<meta>catchword: Lest</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the primary methods used in the early modern period to navigate across water, including bridges, rafts, ferries, and oared boats.</summary>
<keywords>water transport, bridge, raft, ferry-boat, rowing, navigation</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page provides the descriptive text for the illustration found on page 176. It continues the lesson on "Passing over Waters," detailing the engineering and tools used to cross rivers in the 17th century. The text is presented in two columns: the first is the original early modern English, and the second is a translation of the accompanying Latin text.</meta>

<page-num>(177)</page-num>

Lest he that is to passe over a **River** should be all wet, **Bridges** 1. were invented for **Carriages**, and **Foot-bridges** 2. for **Foot-men**.
If a **River** have a **Foord**, 3. <note>A shallow place where water can be crossed by wading.</note> it is waded over. 4.
**Flotes**, 5. <note>Commonly known as rafts.</note> also are made of **Timber** pinned together; or **Ferrie-boats**, 6. of planks laid close together, for fear they should receive water.
Besides **Scullers** 7. <note>Small, light boats usually intended for one or two people.</note> are made, which are rowed with an **Oar**, 8. or a **Pole**, 9. or haled <note>pulled or towed</note> with a **haleing-rope**. 10.

<column-break/>

So that one who is to cross a river may not get wet, **Bridges** 1. <note>original Latin: "Pontes"</note> were devised for **Vehicles**, and **Little-bridges** 2. <note>original Latin: "Ponticuli"</note> for those on **Foot**.
If a river has a **Ford**, 3. <note>original Latin: "Vadum"</note> it is waded across. 4.
**Rafts** 5. <note>original Latin: "Rates"</note> are also constructed from joined logs; or **Pontoons**, 6. <note>original Latin: "Pontones"</note> from planks fastened together, lest they should let in water.
Furthermore, **Skiffs** (or **Small Boats**) 7. <note>original Latin: "Lintres (Lembi)"</note> are built, which are driven by an **Oar**, 8. or a **Pole**, 9. or are pulled by a **Tow-rope**. 10. <note>original Latin: "Remulco"</note>

<sig>N</sig>
<meta>catchword: Swimming <note>original Latin: "Natatus"</note></meta>

<vocab>Bridge <note>Latin: "Pontes"</note>, River <note>Latin: "Flumen"</note>, Ford <note>Latin: "Vadum"</note>, Float or Raft <note>Latin: "Rates"</note>, Ferry-boat <note>Latin: "Pontones"</note>, Sculler or Skiff <note>Latin: "Lintres"</note>, Oar <note>Latin: "Remo"</note>, Pole <note>Latin: "Conto"</note>, Tow-rope <note>Latin: "Remulcus"</note></vocab>

<summary>This page explains various methods for crossing water, ranging from permanent structures like bridges to temporary means like wading, rafts, and small boats. It highlights the distinction between transport for heavy carriages and paths for pedestrians.</summary>
<keywords>bridge, river crossing, ford, raft, ferry, rowing, maritime technology</keywords>

## Swimming.

---

<meta>catchword: Men</meta>
<vocab>Swimming <note>original Latin: Natatus</note></vocab>

<page-num>(177)</page-num>

Lest he that is to passe over a **River** should be all wet, **Bridges** 1. were invented for **Carriages**, and **Foot-bridges** 2. for **Foot-men**.
If a **River** have a **Foord**, 3. <note>A shallow place where water can be crossed by wading.</note> it is waded over. 4.
**Flotes**, 5. <note>Commonly known as rafts.</note> also are made of **Timber** pinned together; or **Ferrie-boats**, 6. of planks laid close together, for fear they should receive water.
Besides **Scullers** 7. <note>Small, light boats usually intended for one or two people.</note> are made, which are rowed with an **Oar**, 8. or a **Pole**, 9. or haled <note>pulled or towed</note> with a **haleing-rope**. 10.

<column-break/>

So that one who is to cross a river may not get wet, **Bridges** 1. <note>original Latin: "Pontes"</note> were devised for **Vehicles**, and **Little-bridges** 2. <note>original Latin: "Ponticuli"</note> for those on **Foot**.
If a river has a **Ford**, 3. <note>original Latin: "Vadum"</note> it is waded across. 4.
**Rafts** 5. <note>original Latin: "Rates"</note> are also constructed from joined logs; or **Pontoons**, 6. <note>original Latin: "Pontones"</note> from planks fastened together, lest they should let in water.
Furthermore, **Skiffs** (or **Small Boats**) 7. <note>original Latin: "Lintres (Lembi)"</note> are built, which are driven by an **Oar**, 8. or a **Pole**, 9. or are pulled by a **Tow-rope**. 10. <note>original Latin: "Remulco"</note>

When a *Storm* 1 <note>original: *Procella*</note>

# The Art of Writing. <note>original: *Ars Scriptoria*</note>

## XCV.

### **The Book-binder.** <note>original Latin: *Bibliopegus*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration in two frames. ### **The Book-binder.**

---

In times past,
they glued
leaves together,
and rolled them into
**Scrolls**. 

Nowadays, the **Book-binder**, 1.
in his **workshop**, 2.
**beats** the **paper**, 3.
upon a **stone**, 4.
with a **hammer**, 5.

Then he **folds** it
into **sheets**, 6.
and **gathers** them
into a **book**, 7.
which he **sews** together, 8.
with **thread**, 9.
upon a **sewing-press**, 10. <note>This device, also known as a sewing frame, keeps the vertical cords or tapes taut while the binder sews the folded sections to them.</note>

Then he **glues** the **back**, 11.
and **trims** it round, 12.
with a **planing-knife**, 13. <note>original: *cultro rotundo*. This refers to the bookbinder’s "plough," a tool used to shear the edges of the pages so they are smooth and even.</note>

Finally, he **covers** it
with **parchment** or **leather**, 14.
beautifies it
with **gilding**, 15.
and attaches **clasps**. 16. <note>Before modern shelf storage, books were often kept lying flat; clasps were essential to keep the parchment pages from swelling or warping due to humidity.</note>

### C.

-># Musical Instruments.<-

**Musical Instruments** are those which make a sound: **First**, when they are beaten upon, as a **Cymbal** 1. <note>original: *Cymbalum*</note> with a **Pestle**, <note>original: *Pistillo*; referring to a striker or mallet.</note> a little **Bell** 2. with an **Iron Pellet** <note>original: *globulo ferreo*; the clapper of the bell.</note> within; a **Rattle**, 3. by tossing it about; a <term>Jew’s-trump</term> 4. <note>Now commonly known as a Jew's harp or jaw harp; a small lyre-shaped instrument held between the teeth.</note> being put to the mouth, with the finger; a **Drum** 5. and a **Kettle[-drum]**, 6. <note>original: *Ahenum*; literally "bronze vessel," referring to the copper body of a timpani.</note> with a **Drum-stick**, 7.

<column-break/>

**Musical instruments** are those which give forth a voice: **First**, when they are struck, such as the **Cymbal** 1. with a **Pestle**, the **Hand-bell** 2. with an **iron pellet** inside; the **Rattle**, 3. by turning it around; the **Jew’s Harp**, 4. moved to the mouth, [played] by the finger; the **Drum** 5. and the **Kettle-drum**, 6. with a **Drum-stick**, 7.

<term>Geometrician</term> — A specialist in geometry, often focused on practical land surveying in this period.
<term>Quadrant</term> — An instrument shaped like a quarter-circle used for measuring altitudes.
<term>Jacob's staff</term> — Also known as a cross-staff, used to measure angles for surveying or navigation.
<term>Square</term> — A "norma" or carpenter's square used to verify 90-degree angles.
<term>Cylinder / Oval</term> — Geometric shapes; the discrepancy between the Latin "Cylinder" and English "Oval" suggests a loose historical grouping of rounded figures.

## Phases of the Moon. <note>original: "Phases Lunæ"</note>

<image-desc>A celestial diagram illustrating the phases of the Moon. At the top, a semi-circle representing the Sun emits rays downward. Below this, a large circle represents the Moon's orbit around a central, shaded sphere (the Earth). Eight positions of the Moon are marked with small circles, numbered 1 through 8. Moon 1 (at the top, closest to the Sun) is entirely dark, representing the New Moon. Moon 5 (at the bottom, furthest from the Sun) is entirely white, representing the Full Moon. Moons 3 and 7 are shown as half-moons (First and Last Quarter). The remaining positions (2, 4, 6, 8) show the transition through crescent and gibbous phases.</image-desc>

# 106.

## *The Eclipses.*

<image-desc>An astronomical diagram contained within a large circle illustrating the mechanics of eclipses. At the top is a large radiant sun with many rays emanating downwards. Positioned directly below the sun are three celestial bodies: 
- Sphere 3 represents the Moon positioned between the Sun and the Earth, casting a conical shadow onto the Earth (a Solar Eclipse). 
- Sphere 1 represents the Earth, which casts a much larger, broad shadow downwards. 
- Sphere 2 represents the Moon as it passes through the Earth's wide shadow (a Lunar Eclipse). 
The number 106 is printed to the left of the main diagram. Below the circle is a horizontal rule and the English translation of the title.</image-desc>

The Sun

### CX. Prudence.

<summary>This page introduces the virtue of Prudence, allegorically depicted as a figure with two faces who uses the lessons of the past and foresight of the future to act with wisdom. **Temperance** 1.
prescribes a measure <note>original: *modum adhibet*</note>
to Food and Drink;
and restrains
the **Appetite**, 2.
as a **Bridle** 3.
does a Horse.
Thus it happens,
that she eats or drinks
nothing in excess. <note>original: *superfluo*</note>

in Diet and Clothing

in Food and Drink

the **Cook's shop** 7.
(or the **Tavern**)

(or the **Vintner**) as a God:
and they worship
the **Kitchen**.

than with wine;

be for a **Cellar** <note>original: *Cella*; specifically a wine-cellar or storage room</note>
to you.

### CXIII.

## Fortitude.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the allegorical figure of Fortitude. A woman dressed in classical armor and a plumed helmet (labeled 1) stands in a hilly landscape. ## Fortitude.

---

<term>Fortitude</term> <note>original: *Fortitudo*; one of the four Cardinal Virtues, representing mental and emotional strength in facing difficulty.</note>, 1.
is undaunted <note>original: *impavida*; fearless or unmoved by terror.</note>
in adversity,
and bold like a **Lion**, 2.
but not **rash** <note>original: *temeraria*; refers to reckless bravery without thought for consequences.</note> or fool-hardy.
Furthermore, she is **constant** <note>original: *constans*; steadfast or unwavering in purpose.</note>
in all things,
and stands upright
like a **Column**, 3.
supported by the
**Shield of Hope**, 4.
against the threats of **Fortune**; <note>In this context, "Fortune" refers to the unpredictable changes of luck or fate, often personified as a fickle goddess.</note>
she also bears
her misfortunes
patiently.

<column-break/>

*Fortitude 1.*
*is undaunted in adversity,* <note>original: *in adversis impavida est*</note>
*and bold as a Lion, 2.* <note>The lion is a traditional symbol of courage and majesty in Western heraldry and allegory.</note>
*but not reckless.*
*Moreover, she is steadfast in all things,*
*and upright as a Column, 3.* <note>The column represents stability and the ability to bear weight without breaking.</note>
*leaning upon the Shield of Hope, 4.* <note>original: *innixa Clypeo Spei*; suggesting that hope is the defensive tool that protects the soul from despair.</note>
*against the threats of Fate;*
*She also suffers*
*ill-fortune*
*with patience.* <note>original: *Infortunia patienter*; emphasizing that fortitude is not just about active bravery, but also passive endurance.</note>

<summary>This page defines Fortitude as a balance between fear and recklessness, using the symbols of a lion for courage and a column for stability. It characterizes the virtuous person as someone who remains steadfast against the whims of fate, protected by hope.</summary>

<keywords>Fortitude, courage, constancy, hope, allegory, lion, column, cardinal virtues, adversity</keywords>

so that he may always have:
But the *Prodigal*, 12. <note>original: "Prodigus"</note>
badly spends things
well-earned,
and at last is in want.
