## CXXVI.

### *Merchandizing.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a commercial scene or marketplace. On the left, a group of four men wearing wide-brimmed hats and long cloaks are gathered near a large arched stone entrance. In the center foreground, two figures interact across a wooden table. To the right, a merchant stands behind a wooden stall with a small roof, and another figure is seated at a nearby counter. The background shows a building with multiple shuttered windows. The numeral "126" is printed in the bottom-left corner of the illustration.</image-desc>

### **Merchandizing.**

---

Wares <note>original: "Merces"</note> are those things which are brought from one place to another, and are sold for the sake of profit.

The Merchant <note>original: "Mercator"</note> provides these things, and brings them to the Market-place, or to a Shop, where he exposes them to sale.

He buys them at a lower price from the producer, and sells them at a higher price to the buyer, that he may gain a living for himself and his family.

<margin>The balance or scales</margin>
He weighs his goods in a pair of scales, or measures them with a yard-wand <note>A traditional unit of length, roughly 3 feet</note>, so that he may give to every man his due.

<insert>The Shop-keeper</insert> sits in his stall and waits for customers, whom he invites with fair words to look at his goods.

<gloss>Money is the measure of all things</gloss>

He receives money <note>original: "Pecunia"</note>, which is the price of the goods, and enters the account into his Ledger or Book of Debts.

<column-break/>

If he sells on credit, he trusts the buyer until a certain day, but he must be careful lest he lose his capital through bad debtors.

The Wholesale Merchant <note>original: "Emporus"</note> travels to distant lands, even across the sea, to bring back foreign goods which are not found in his own country.

He carries his goods in ships, or upon beasts of burden, such as horses and camels, through long and dangerous journeys.

<margin>The Warehouse</margin>
He stores his wealth in a Warehouse, until the time is right to distribute it to the lesser traders.

Thus, through the labor of the Merchant, the riches of the whole world are shared among all men, and no one lacks what is necessary for life.

<summary>This page describes the fundamental principles of trade and commerce, detailing the roles of local shopkeepers and international wholesale merchants in the distribution of goods.</summary>
<keywords>merchandizing, trade, merchant, commerce, scales, money, ledger, wholesale, imports</keywords>

### CXXIX.

## The Burial.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed by a border, numbered 129 in the bottom right corner. It depicts a burial scene within a stone structure, likely a crypt or church. In the center, a corpse lies on a raised bier or platform (labeled 4, 5, 7). Behind the bier stands a figure in a hood, possibly a priest, holding a staff. Groups of mourners or clergy members dressed in long, dark, hooded cloaks stand to the left (6) and right. In the background, a vessel sits on a shelf (1), and the vaulted ceiling is visible (10). On the right wall, a bell hangs within a masonry alcove. Numbers 1, 4, 5, 6, 7, and 10 are placed throughout the image to correlate with a descriptive text.</image-desc>

## A Burial.

---

Dead bodies <note>original: "Defuncti"</note> were formerly wrapped in **winding-sheets** (1) <note>A cloth used to wrap a body for burial, also called a shroud.</note> and were carried out upon a **bier** (2) to the **burial-place** (3).

Nowadays they are put into a **coffin** (4), and are carried upon a **hand-bier** (5) <note>A portable frame used by pallbearers to carry the coffin to the grave.</note> by the **bearers** (6) to the **church-yard** (7).

There they are buried in a **grave** (8) by the **sexton** (9) <note>A church official maintained the church buildings and dug the graves.</note>, and are covered with earth.

The **monuments** (10) <note>original: "Monumenta"</note> are placed above them, such as **gravestones** or **tombs**, to keep their memory alive.

The **mourners** (11) follow the corpse in a long procession, dressed in black, to show their grief.

<summary>This page describes the traditional and contemporary customs of burial, detailing the transition from simple shrouds and biers to the use of coffins, churchyards, and permanent stone monuments.</summary>
<keywords>burial, funeral rites, coffin, sexton, monuments, mourning, churchyard</keywords>

### CXXX.

## *A Stage-Play.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a theatrical stage performance. In the foreground, a large audience of people is shown from behind, looking toward the stage. The stage is raised and framed by heavy, draped curtains at the top and sides. On the stage, several actors are performing a scene; one figure on the left appears to be gesturing dramatically, while another in the center is seated or leaning over a low structure. Numerical labels (1, 2, 4, 6, 8) are positioned near various elements of the scene (audience, actors, stage). The background of the stage shows painted scenery, including stylized trees and a stone building facade.</image-desc>

## **A Stage-Play.**

---

In a **Play-house** (which is trimmed with **Hangings** (1) and covered with **Curtains** (2)), **Comedies** and **Tragedies** are acted, wherein memorable things are represented; as here, the History of the *Prodigal Son* (3) and his Father (4) by whom he is entertained, being returned home.

The **Players** act being disguised with **Apparel** (5) and **Masks** <note>original: "Larvis"</note>.

The **Master of the Revels** (6) <note>The person overseeing the production or the prompter</note> suggesteth the matters out of a **Writing**.

The **Spectators** (7) sit on **Galleries** <note>original: "Orchestra"</note> and **Benches** (8) and behold the Play.

<column-break/>

In a **Theater** (which is adorned with **Tapestry** (1) and veiled with **Curtains** (2)), **Plays** and **Tragedies** are performed, in which notable events are shown; as here, the Story of the *Lost Son* (3) and his Father (4) by whom he is received, having come back.

The **Actors** perform being dressed in **Costumes** (5) and **False-faces** <note>original: "Personis"</note>.

The **Prompter** (6) whispers the words out of a **Book**.

The **Audience** (7) sit upon **Tiers** and **Seats** (8) and watch the Performance.

<summary>This page describes the components of a theatrical production, including the stage setting, the roles of actors and prompters, and the arrangement of the audience, using the parable of the Prodigal Son as an example.</summary>
<keywords>theater, stage-play, comedy, tragedy, actors, audience, drama, prodigal son</keywords>

### CXXXI.

## *Sleights of Hand and Feats of Agility.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration labeled with the number "131" in the lower-left corner. It depicts various forms of entertainment and physical feats. On the left, a woman balances on a tightrope using a long pole, while below her, a man performs acrobatics, hanging upside down from a frame of wooden poles. In the center, a man stands on a raised wooden stage; at his feet, a small figure (likely a monkey or a child) leaps through a hoop. In the background of the stage, another person hangs upside down from a horizontal bar. To the right, a man sits at a table performing "cups and balls" sleight-of-hand tricks.</image-desc>

## Sleights.

---

The **Juggler** <note>original: "Præstigiator"</note> shows various **Sleights** and wonders from his **Amusement-box**. 

The first kind of these feats are performed by **Sleight of hand**: he hides a **Little Ball** <note>original: "Calculum"</note> under a **Cup**, and then by a quick motion of his fingers, he conveys it into another, or makes it vanish entirely. He also draws **Ribbons** out of his mouth, or performs other tricks to deceive the eyes of the beholders.

<column-break/>

The second kind consists of **Feats of Agility**. The **Rope-dancer** <note>original: "Funambulus"</note> walks upon a **Tightrope**, holding a **Balancing-pole** in his hand to maintain his equilibrium; or he climbs up a **Slanting-rope**. 

The **Tumbler** <note>original: "Grallator"</note> or **Acrobat** <note>original: "Petaurista"</note> shows his strength by swinging from a **Bar**, or by leaping through a **Hoop**. Sometimes a **Vaulting-master** <note>original: "Desultor"</note> performs leaps over horses or through the air. 

The **Stilt-walker** <note>original: "Grallator"</note> <note>Note: The Latin term "Grallator" is used for both tumblers and those on stilts</note> walks upon high **Stilts** above the heads of the crowd. These performers often travel from town to town to entertain the people at fairs.

<summary>This page describes various forms of early modern entertainment, including sleight-of-hand magic, tightrope walking, and acrobatics. It categorizes these performances into tricks of the hand and physical feats of agility.</summary>
<keywords>juggling, magic tricks, rope-dancing, acrobatics, stilts, public entertainment, sleight of hand</keywords>

# CXXXII.

## *The Fencing School.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a fencing school. Several men are shown in a stone-walled courtyard or training hall, practicing with various weapons. In the foreground, two pairs of fencers are actively engaged in combat practice. Several swords and a polearm lie on the floor. The scene is annotated with small numbers (1 through 8) placed near the figures and the equipment on the ground, intended to correspond with descriptive text.</image-desc>

## **The Fencing School.**

---

Fencers **1** <note>original: "Pugiles"</note> practice in a **Fencing School**, fighting one another with **Foil-swords 2** <note>original: "Rudibus"; these were blunt wooden or leather-covered swords used for safe practice</note>, or with **Broad-swords 3** <note>original: "Ensibus"</note>, and **Daggers 4**.

They use either the **edge** or the **point** <note>original: "punctim & cæsim"; a technical fencing term for thrusting and cutting</note> of the weapon.

They parry the blows of their adversaries with **Shields 5** <note>original: "Clypeis"</note> or **Bucklers 6**.

In former times, they fought with **Whirl-bats 7** <note>original: "Cæstibus"; ancient Roman boxing gloves weighted with lead or iron</note> and **Clubs 8**.

<summary>This page describes the equipment and techniques used in a 17th-century fencing school, contrasting contemporary swordplay with ancient methods of combat.</summary>

<keywords>fencing, martial arts, swords, shields, combat training, historical weaponry</keywords>

( 272 )

### CXXXIV.

## The Game of Dice.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a room prepared for various games. In the foreground, a large table holds a chessboard (labeled 1) and a game of "tables" or backgammon (labeled 2). A smaller table in the background features a circular game board or wheel (labeled 3) with a tiered centerpiece. To the right, a long wooden bench (labeled 6) sits beneath two windows; several small dice (labeled 5) are shown on the bench.</image-desc>

## Dice-Play.

---

We play with <term>Dice</term> <note>original: "tesserae"; small cubes used for gambling or games of chance</note> upon a <term>Gaming-board</term> <note>original: "fritillus"; a dice-box or the board itself</note>, or we play at <term>Tables</term> <note>An early form of backgammon played with counters and dice</note> with <term>Men</term> <note>The playing pieces or counters</note> upon a <term>Pair of Tables</term>.

We also play at <term>Chess</term> <note>original: "Latrunculi"; literally "little soldiers," the Roman name for a strategy game similar to chess</note> upon a <term>Checkered-board</term>, where only wit <note>meaning mental skill or strategy</note> bears the sway.

The <term>Lottery</term> <note>original: "pittacia"; small slips of paper or tickets used for drawing lots</note> is a game of chance, where we draw <term>Lots</term> out of a <term>Pitcher</term> or <term>Box</term> to see what fortune shall provide.

<summary>This page illustrates and describes various indoor games of skill and chance, including dice, backgammon, chess, and the drawing of lots.</summary>
<keywords>dice, chess, backgammon, games of chance, lottery, recreation, 17th-century pastimes</keywords>

# - 135.

## Competitive Races.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting various competitive races and games, numbered for reference. On the left side, several men in tunics are shown running a footrace (1) along a track bounded by wooden rails. In the background, figures are active on a frozen body of water: one is skating (2) and another is being moved on a sled (3). On the right, a man is positioned near a wooden frame for a ball game, with pins (6, 7) and balls (8, 9). A tall post with a ring (11) stands on the far left. The scene captures a variety of physical contests including running, winter sports, and bowling.</image-desc>

### Races.

---

<meta>The previous page likely discussed physical exercises or games; this page continues the theme by focusing on competitive speed and skill.</meta>

**Boys** <note>original: "Pueri"</note> used to exercise themselves by running, either together in a **Foot-race** (1), where a prize is proposed for the one who outruns the rest; or by leaping over a bar, or by jumping with their feet joined together.

In the Winter-time, they exercise themselves by **Skating** (2) upon the ice, using wooden pattens <note>An early form of ice skates with wooden bases and iron blades.</note> or the leg-bones of oxen; or they are moved along upon a **Sled** (3).

In the Summer-time, they used to play with a **Ball** (4), either tossing it into the air with a Racket (5), or striking it with a bat; or they play at **Nine-pins** (6), where they aim at the pins (7) with a **Bowl** (8); or they throw a ball through an **Iron Ring** (11) <note>original: "Annulum ferreum"; likely a reference to a game similar to croquet or pall-mall.</note> with a mallet (9).

These types of **Contests** <note>original: "Certamina"</note> serve not only for the recreation of the mind, but also for the strengthening of the body.

<summary>This page describes various physical competitions and recreational games for youth, ranging from foot-races and winter ice-skating to ball games and bowling.</summary>
<keywords>races, physical education, skating, sledding, ball games, bowling, recreation</keywords>

### CXXXVII.

## Kingdom & Region.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a wide landscape representing a kingdom and its regions. The scene includes hills, rivers, and various settlements. On the far left hill is a castle or fortress (labeled with a faint 1). In the center-right is another hilltop castle (labeled 2). A walled town sits in the middle of the valley. In the foreground, a river flows, and a small rowing boat with a figure is visible (labeled 4). Scattered throughout the landscape are smaller buildings, churches, and houses.</image-desc>

## The Kingdom & the Region.

---

Many **Regions** together make up a **Kingdom** <note>original: "Regnum"</note>.
The **Head** of the Kingdom is the **King**;
the **Heart** of the Kingdom is the **Capital City** <note>original: "Metropolis"</note>.
In a **Region** <note>original: "Regio"</note> there are:
**Castles** (1) <note>fortified residences for nobility</note> built upon hills;
**Fortresses** (2) <note>original: "Arces"</note> situated on high places;
**Cities** (3) <note>original: "Civitates"</note> surrounded by walls;
and **Towns** <note>original: "Oppida"</note> which are smaller than cities.
In the open country are **Villages** <note>original: "Pagi"</note> and **Hamlets** <note>original: "Vici"</note>.
The **Fields** are watered by **Rivers** (4) and **Brooks**.
The **Highways** <note>original: "Viae Regiae"</note> lead from one city to another.
The **Borders** <note>original: "Confinia"</note> separate one kingdom from another.

<summary>This page defines the constituent parts of a kingdom, ranging from the capital city and fortified castles to rural villages and the infrastructure of rivers and roads.</summary>
<keywords>Kingdom, Region, City, Castle, Fortress, Geography, Political Organization</keywords>

If we are to make war,
Soldiers 1. are enlisted. <note>original: "scribuntur Milites" - literally "written down" or enrolled in the registers.</note>

(which is adorned with
a Crest)

Arm-pieces, 5. <note>original: "Brachialia"</note>
iron Leg-pieces, 6. <note>original: "Ocreæ ferreæ"</note>
Greaves or Gauntlets, 7. <note>original: "Manicæ" - while the English says greaves (leg protection), the Latin "manicae" specifically refers to sleeves or hand protection.</note>
with a Coat of Mail, 8. <note>original: "Lorica"</note>
and a Shield or Buckler, 9. <note>original: "Scuto (Clypeo)"</note>

### 140.

## *The Camp.*

<image-desc>A detailed engraving of a military encampment and battle formation. Various units are numbered for identification: infantry blocks are arranged in the foreground and middle ground (1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 9); cavalry units are positioned on the right flank (8, 10); a line of artillery cannons is set up in the center (14); drummers and musicians are visible on the right (12). ## **The Camps.**

---

When a <term>Levy</term> <note>The formal process of enrolling or drafting soldiers into military service</note> is made, the soldiers are gathered together and distributed into <term>Regiments</term> <note>Large military units typically commanded by a colonel</note>. 

The <term>Infantry</term> <note>original: "Peditatus" - soldiers who fight on foot</note> (1) are divided into companies, which are led by a Captain. These companies carry their own colors or ensigns. 

The <term>Cavalry</term> <note>original: "Equitatus" - soldiers who fight on horseback</note> (8) are divided into troops, led by a Commander or Captain of the Horse. 

The <term>Artillery</term> <note>original: "Tormenta" - heavy weaponry such as cannons</note> (14) consists of the great guns and cannons, managed by the gunners. 

When the army is on the march, the <term>Baggage Train</term> <note>original: "Impedimenta" - the wagons and supplies that follow an army</note> (19) follows behind with the provisions and tents. 

When they reach the place of rest, they pitch their **Camps**. The camp is fortified with a trench and a rampart to protect the soldiers from sudden attacks. The <term>Sentinels</term> <note>Guards stationed to keep watch</note> stand at the gates (17) to watch for the enemy. 

The musicians, such as the drummers (12) and trumpeters, signal the orders of the General to the whole army.

<summary>This page describes the organization of an early modern army, detailing the divisions of infantry, cavalry, and artillery, as well as the structure of a fortified military camp.</summary>
<keywords>military organization, infantry, cavalry, artillery, encampment, fortifications, 17th-century warfare</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the pedagogical sequence of the Orbis Sensualium Pictus, moving from earthly life to the final theological conclusion of history.</meta>

### 150.

## **The Last Judgment.**

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of the Last Judgment. In the upper center, Christ sits in majesty upon a globe (marked with the number 3) within a radiant circular glory. He is flanked by clouds from which two angels emerge, blowing long trumpets (labeled 1 and 7). Below, in the earthly realm, figures are seen rising from their graves (labeled 2 and 4). To the left, a group of the righteous (5) gather near a structure, while on the right, the wicked (6) are being cast into the fiery, monstrous maw of hell (8).</image-desc>

## **The last Judgement.**

---

At the last day, the **Sun** and **Moon** shall be darkened, and the **Stars** shall fall upon the earth; then the **Sign of the Son of Man** <note>original: "Signum Filii Hominis"; likely referring to the Cross appearing in the sky</note> shall appear.

And at the sound of the **Trumpet** (1), the **Dead** shall rise again (2), and those who are then alive shall be changed in a moment.

Then **Christ** (3), the Judge of the world, shall sit upon his **Throne** of Majesty, and all **Nations** shall be gathered before him.

He shall separate the **Good** (4) from the **Bad** (6), as a shepherd separates the sheep from the goats.

The **Godly** (5) he shall place on his right hand, and the **Ungodly** (6) on his left.

Then he shall pass sentence, saying to the **Blessed**: "Come, ye blessed of my Father, inherit the kingdom prepared for you."

But to the **Cursed** he shall say: "Depart from me, ye cursed, into everlasting fire, prepared for the Devil and his angels."

Then the **Godly** shall go into **Eternal Life**, but the **Wicked** shall be cast into **Eternal Punishment** (8) <note>original: "Supplicium aeternum"</note> with the **Devils** (7) into the lake of fire and brimstone.

<summary>This page depicts the Christian theological concept of the Last Judgment, illustrating the resurrection of the dead and the final separation of the righteous from the wicked by Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, Resurrection, Christ as Judge, Salvation, Damnation, End of Days, Heaven and Hell</keywords>

<meta>This is the title page of a 17th-century language manual. The annotations indicate it is part of the Thomason Tracts collection, a famous repository of English Civil War-era publications.</meta>

<insert>1598 <unclear>crossed out</unclear> C. 21. a. 16 2</insert> <note>These are likely library shelfmarks or cataloging numbers added by later collectors.</note>

# THE
# Dutch-Tutor:
<insert>K</insert> <note>Likely a collector's mark or signature letter.</note>

## OR,
## A NEW BOOK
### OF
## *Dutch and English.*

### CONTAINING
Plain and easy Rules for the ready
Pronouncing, Writing, Speaking,
and understanding the
Dutch Tongue. <note>In this period, "Dutch" often referred generally to Germanic languages, but here specifically refers to the language of the Netherlands.</note>

Compiled for the benefit and furtherance of all that would easily and speedily learn the same.

->LONDON,<-
->Printed for *William Fisher*, and are<-
->sold at his Shop at the Postern-gate<-
<insert>Feb:</insert> ->near Tower-hill.<-

<insert>1658. Feb. 24</insert> <note>This handwritten date was likely added by George Thomason, the bookseller who attempted to collect every publication issued during the English Revolution.</note>
